We determine the set of clauses that are unsatisfied by
\widehat{X}
or
\widehat{Y}
with the help of Proposition
41
. We distinguish 3 disjoint cases:

•

c\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{rem}}
. Then
\operatorname{var}(c)\cap\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{d}}=\emptyset
, so
\widehat{X}
and
\widehat{Y}
agree in all the variables in
\operatorname{var}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{rem}})\cap\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{set}}
. As a consequence, the restrictions of
\Phi^{\widehat{X}}
and
\Phi^{\widehat{Y}}
to
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{rem}}
give rise to the same CNF formula. Note that some of the clauses in
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{rem}}
might be satisfied by both
\widehat{X}
and
\widehat{Y}
, but they are never satisfied by only one of the two assignments.

•

c\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{u}}
. Then by Property
2
of Proposition
41
,
c
unsatisfied by at least one of
\widehat{X}
and
\widehat{Y}
and, thus, it appears in at least one of
\Phi^{\widehat{X}}
and
\Phi^{\widehat{Y}}
. The clause
c
may contain a variable
v\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{d}}
, in which case
c
is a good clause and it appears in exactly one of the formulas
\Phi^{\widehat{X}}
and
\Phi^{\widehat{Y}}
.

•

c\in\mathcal{C}\setminus(\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{rem}}\cup\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{u}})
. By Proposition
41
, we have
\operatorname{var}(c)\cap(\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{d}}\cup\operatorname{var}(\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{u}}))\neq\emptyset
and
\operatorname{var}(c)\cap\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{a}}\subseteq\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{set}}
. Since
c\not\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{u}}
, it follows that
c
is satisfied by both
\widehat{X}
and
\widehat{Y}
and, thus,
c
does not appear in any of the formulae
\Phi^{\widehat{X}}
and
\Phi^{\widehat{Y}}
.

We conclude that we can write
\mathcal{C}^{\widehat{X}}=\mathcal{C}_{1}\cup\mathcal{C}_{2}
and
\mathcal{C}^{\widehat{Y}}=\mathcal{C}_{1}\cup\mathcal{C}_{3}
, where
\mathcal{C}_{1}\subseteq\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{rem}}
and
\mathcal{C}_{2},\mathcal{C}_{3}\subseteq\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{u}}
as we wanted.
∎

In order to further analyse the probability distribution of the output of Algorithm
4
, we introduce the following definition.

Definition 43

(run,
\mathcal{R}(\Phi,\mathcal{M},u,\Lambda)
,
\tau_{\mathcal{R}}(\Phi,\mathcal{M},u,\Lambda)
,
\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{set}}(R)
,
\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{d}}(R)
,
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{u}}(R)
,
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{d}}(R)
,
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{rem}}(R)
)
.

A
run
of Algorithm
4
is a sequence of all the random choices
(\widehat{X}(v),\widehat{Y}(v))
made in line
17
when executing Algorithm
4
. Let
\mathcal{R}(\Phi,\mathcal{M},u,\Lambda)
be the set of all possible runs of Algorithm
4
for the inputs
\Phi,\mathcal{M},u,\Lambda
and let
\tau_{\mathcal{R}}(\Phi,\mathcal{M},u,\Lambda)
be the probability distribution that Algorithm
4
yields on
\mathcal{R}(\Phi,\mathcal{M},u,\Lambda)
. Each run
R\in\mathcal{R}(\Phi,\mathcal{M},u,\Lambda)
determines the output
(\widehat{X},\widehat{Y})
and the sets
\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{set}}(R),\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{d}}(R),\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{u}}(R),\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{d}}(R),\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{rem}}(R)
that are computed in Algorithm
4
.

With the aim of applying Proposition
38
, we extend the coupling
(\widehat{X},\widehat{Y})
to all marked and auxiliary variables.

Definition 44

(The coupling
(X,Y)
)
.

Let
R\in\mathcal{R}(\Phi,\mathcal{M},u,\Lambda)
and let
(\widehat{X},\widehat{Y})
be the corresponding output of the run
R
. Let
\leq_{\mathcal{V}}
be a total order on the variables of
\Phi
and let
v_{1}\leq_{\mathcal{V}}v_{2}\leq_{\mathcal{V}}\dots\leq_{\mathcal{V}}v_{t}
be the variables in
(\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}\cup\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{a}})\setminus\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{set}}
. We extend the assignments
\widehat{X},\widehat{Y}\colon\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{set}}\to\{\mathsf{F},\mathsf{T}\}
to
v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{t}
inductively (as follows) to obtain a coupling
(X,Y)
such that
X
follows the distribution
{\left.\kern-1.2pt\mu_{\Omega^{\Lambda\cup u\mapsto\mathsf{T}}}\right|_{(\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}\cup\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{a}})\setminus\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{set}}}}
and
Y
follows the distribution
{\left.\kern-1.2pt\mu_{\Omega^{\Lambda\cup u\mapsto\mathsf{F}}}\right|_{(\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}\cup\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{a}})\setminus\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{set}}}}
. Assume that
X
and
Y
are defined on
\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{set}}\cup\{v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{j-1}\}
for
j\in\{1,2,\ldots,t\}
. Then we sample
(X(v_{j}),Y(v_{j}))
from the optimal/monotone coupling of the marginal distributions (on
v_{j}
) of
\mu_{\Omega^{X}}
and
\mu_{\Omega^{Y}}
.

Remark 45
.

When
R\in\mathcal{R}(\Phi,\mathcal{M},u,\Lambda)
follows the probability distribution
\tau_{\mathcal{R}}(\Phi,\mathcal{M},u,\Lambda)
(Definition
43
), the pair of random assignments
(X,Y)
of Definition
44
is a coupling of the distributions
{\left.\kern-1.2pt\mu_{\Omega^{\Lambda\cup u\mapsto\mathsf{T}}}\right|_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}\cup\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{a}}}}
and
{\left.\kern-1.2pt\mu_{\Omega^{\Lambda\cup u\mapsto\mathsf{F}}}\right|_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}\cup\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{a}}}}
.

In Lemma
46
we bound the probabilities
\operatorname{Pr}(X(v)\neq Y(v)|R)
for any
R\in\mathcal{R}(\Phi,\mathcal{M},u,\Lambda)
and
v\in(\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}\cup\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{a}})\setminus\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{set}}(R)
.

Lemma 46
.

Let
R\in\mathcal{R}(\Phi,\mathcal{M},u,\Lambda)
. Let
(X,Y)
be the coupling of Definition
44
. Then for any
v\in(\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}\cup\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{a}})\setminus\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{set}}(R)
we have
\operatorname{Pr}(X(v)\neq Y(v)|R)\leq 2^{-r_{0}k+1}/k
.

Proof.

Let
\widehat{X}
and
\widehat{Y}
be the output of Algorithm
4
for the run
R
. Let
v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{t}
be the variables in
(\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}\cup\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{a}})\setminus\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{set}}(R)
in the order that they are considered in Definition
44
. Let
j\in\{1,2,\ldots,t\}
and let
\Lambda^{\prime},\Lambda^{\prime\prime}\colon\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{set}}(R)\cup\{v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{j-1}\}\to\{\mathsf{F},\mathsf{T}\}
be two assignments such that
{\left.\kern-1.2pt\Lambda^{\prime}\right|_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{set}}}}=\widehat{X}
and
{\left.\kern-1.2pt\Lambda^{\prime\prime}\right|_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{set}}}}=\widehat{Y}
. When
X
agrees with
\Lambda^{\prime}
and
Y
agrees with
\Lambda^{\prime\prime}
, the values
X(v_{j})
and
Y(v_{j})
are sampled from the optimal/monotone coupling between the marginals on
v_{j}
of the distributions
\mu_{\Omega^{\Lambda^{\prime}}}
and
\mu_{\Omega^{\Lambda^{\prime\prime}}}
. Let us denote these marginals by
\nu_{X}
and
\nu_{Y}
respectively. Thus, by the coupling lemma (Proposition
37
) and Proposition
10
(or Lemma
28
) on the marginals of marked and auxiliary variables, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{Pr}\left(X(v_{j})\neq Y(v_{j})|\Lambda^{\prime},\Lambda^{\prime\prime}\right)

\displaystyle=d_{\TV}(\nu_{X},\nu_{Y})=\left|\operatorname{Pr}(X(v_{j})=\mathsf{T}|\Lambda^{\prime})-\operatorname{Pr}(Y(v_{j})=\mathsf{T}|\Lambda^{\prime\prime})\right|

\displaystyle\leq\lvert\operatorname{Pr}(X(v_{j})=\mathsf{T}|\Lambda^{\prime})-1/2\rvert+\lvert 1/2-\operatorname{Pr}(Y(v_{j})=\mathsf{T}|\Lambda^{\prime\prime})\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(\frac{1}{k2^{r_{0}k}}\right)-1.

Applying the inequality
e^{z}\leq 1+2z
for
z\in(0,1)
, we find that
\operatorname{Pr}\left(X(v_{j})\neq Y(v_{j})|\Lambda^{\prime},\Lambda^{\prime\prime}\right)\leq 2^{-r_{0}k+1}/k
. Thus, from the arbitrary choice of
\Lambda^{\prime},\Lambda^{\prime\prime}
and the law of total probability we conclude that the bound
\operatorname{Pr}\left(X(v_{j})\neq Y(v_{j})|R\right)\leq 2^{-r_{0}k+1}/k
holds.
∎

Combining all the results presented up to this stage in the current section allows us relate the sum
\sum_{v\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}\setminus(S\cup\{u\})}\left|\mathcal{I}^{\Lambda}(u\to v)\right|
to the coupling process over auxiliary variables. In fact, we bound this sum of influences between marked variables by the expected number of failed clauses in the coupling process on auxiliary variables. Recall that here
r=r_{0}-\delta
.

Lemma 47
.

There is an integer
k_{0}
such that for any
k\geq k_{0}
and any density
\alpha
with
\alpha\leq 2^{(r_{0}-\delta)k}/k^{3}
the following holds w.h.p. over the choice of the random
k
-CNF formula
\Phi=\Phi(k,n,\lfloor\alpha n\rfloor)
. Let
(\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}},\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{a}},\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{c}})
be an
(r_{0}-\delta,r_{0},r_{0},2r_{0})
-marking of
\Phi
, and let
u\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}
and
\Lambda\colon S\to\{\mathsf{F},\mathsf{T}\}
with
S\subseteq\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}\setminus\{u\}
. Then for a random run
R
of the coupling process on the auxiliary variables (Algorithm
4
), we have

\sum_{v\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}\setminus(S\cup\{u\})}\left|\mathcal{I}^{\Lambda}(u\to v)\right|\leq 2^{-r_{0}k+1}\mathbb{E}\left[\lvert\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{u}}(R)\rvert\right].

Proof.

Let
(X,Y)
be the coupling in Definition
44
for a (random) run
R\sim\tau_{\mathcal{R}}(\Phi,\mathcal{M},u,\Lambda)
of Algorithm
4
.
We are going to show that

\operatorname{Pr}(X(v)=Y(v)|R)=1\text{ for all }v\in V:=(\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}\cup\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{a}})\setminus(\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{set}}(R)\cup\operatorname{var}(\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{u}}(R))).

(27)

Let
\widehat{X},\widehat{Y}\colon\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{set}}(R)\to\{\mathsf{F},\mathsf{T}\}
be the output of Algorithm
4
for the run
R
. By Lemma
42
we conclude that we can write
\mathcal{C}^{\widehat{X}}=\mathcal{C}_{1}\cup\mathcal{C}_{2}
and
\mathcal{C}^{\widehat{Y}}=\mathcal{C}_{1}\cup\mathcal{C}_{3}
, where
\mathcal{C}_{1}\subseteq\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{rem}}(R)
and
\mathcal{C}_{2},\mathcal{C}_{3}\subseteq\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{u}}(R)
. Thus, the variables in
V
(see (
27
) for a definition of
V
) either appear in a clause in
\mathcal{C}_{1}
or they are not present in any of the formulae
\Phi^{\widehat{X}}
and
\Phi^{\widehat{Y}}
. Moreover, by Proposition
41
, we have
\operatorname{var}(c)\cap\operatorname{var}(c^{\prime})=\emptyset
for all
c\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{rem}}(R)
and
c^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{u}}(R)
. We conclude that the distributions
{\left.\kern-1.2pt\mu_{\Omega^{\widehat{X}}}\right|_{V}}
and
{\left.\kern-1.2pt\mu_{\Omega^{\widehat{Y}}}\right|_{V}}
agree – both are the uniform distribution over the satisfying assignments of the CNF formula
(V,\mathcal{C}_{1})
. Let
v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{t}
be the variables in
V
in the order they are considered in the definition of the coupling
(X,Y)
. By induction on
j\in\{1,2,\ldots,t\}
, the marginals on
v_{j}
in Definition
44
are the same when coupling
X(v_{j})
and
Y(v_{j})
. Thus, we have
X(v_{j})=Y(v_{j})
for all
j\in\{1,2,\ldots,t\}
.

Since
S\cup\{u\}\subseteq\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{set}}(R)\subseteq S\cup\{u\}\cup\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{a}}
, we have
\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}\setminus V=S\cup\{u\}\cup(\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}\cap\operatorname{var}(\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{u}}(R)))
. In light of Lemma
46
and (
27
), we find that

\sum_{v\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}\setminus(S\cup\{u\})}\operatorname{Pr}(X(v)\neq Y(v)|R)\leq\sum_{v\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}\cap\operatorname{var}(\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{u}}(R))}\operatorname{Pr}(X(v)\neq Y(v)|R)\leq\frac{2}{k}2^{-r_{0}k}\lvert\operatorname{var}(\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{u}}(R))\rvert.

From
\lvert\operatorname{var}(\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{u}}(R))\rvert\leq k\lvert\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{u}}(R)\rvert
we conclude that

\sum_{v\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}\setminus(S\cup\{u\})}\operatorname{Pr}(X(v)\neq Y(v)|R)\leq 2^{-r_{0}k+1}\lvert\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{u}}(R)\rvert.

(28)

In the rest of this proof we are going to aggregate (
28
) over
R\in\mathcal{R}(\Phi,\mathcal{M},u,\Lambda)
with the aim of applying Proposition
38
. Let
(X,Y)
be the coupling in Definition
44
for a (random) run
R\sim\tau_{\mathcal{R}}(\Phi,\mathcal{M},u,\Lambda)
of Algorithm
4
. We have

\displaystyle\sum_{v\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}\setminus(S\cup\{u\})}\operatorname{Pr}(X(v)\neq Y(v))

\displaystyle=\sum_{v\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}\setminus(S\cup\{u\})}\sum_{R\in\mathcal{R}(\Phi,\mathcal{M},u,\Lambda)}\operatorname{Pr}(R)\operatorname{Pr}(X(v)\neq Y(v)|R)

\displaystyle=\sum_{R\in\mathcal{R}(\Phi,\mathcal{M},u,\Lambda)}\operatorname{Pr}(R)\sum_{v\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}\setminus(S\cup\{u\})}\operatorname{Pr}(X(v)\neq Y(v)|R)

\displaystyle\leq 2^{-r_{0}k+1}\sum_{R\in\mathcal{R}(\Phi,\mathcal{M},u,\Lambda)}\operatorname{Pr}(R)\lvert\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{u}}(R)\rvert

\displaystyle=2^{-r_{0}k+1}\mathbb{E}\left[\lvert\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{u}}(R)\rvert\right].

Finally, we note that we can indeed apply Proposition
38
to the restriction of
X
and
Y
on
\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}
as
(X,Y)
is a coupling of the distributions
{\left.\kern-1.2pt\mu_{\Omega^{\Lambda\cup u\mapsto\mathsf{T}}}\right|_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}\cup\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{a}}}}
and
{\left.\kern-1.2pt\mu_{\Omega^{\Lambda\cup u\mapsto\mathsf{F}}}\right|_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}\cup\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{a}}}}
(Remark
45
). This finishes the proof.
∎

In the remainder of this section we bound
\mathbb{E}\left[\lvert\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{u}}(R)\rvert\right]
, which would complete our proof of Lemma
14
when combined with Lemma
47
. In order to do this we exploit the fact that
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{u}}(R)\cup\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{d}}(R)
is connected in
G_{\Phi}
(Proposition
41
), the local sparsity properties of random CNF formulae and the properties of the marking
(\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}},\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{a}},\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{c}})
. It is important that the bound on
\mathbb{E}\left[\lvert\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{u}}(R)\rvert\right]
is
\operatorname{poly}(k)\log n
in order to conclude fast mixing time of the
\rho
-uniform-block Glauber dynamics when applying the spectral independence framework. First, we bound the probability that some good clauses are failed in Algorithm
4
. At first glance this seems to be a straightforward task thanks to the fact that the marginals of marked and auxiliary variables are close to
1/2
(see Proposition
10
). However, for any good clauses
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
, the events that
c_{1}\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{d}}(R)\cup\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{u}}(R)
and
c_{2}\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{d}}(R)\cup\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{u}}(R)
may not be independent; any value given to the variables in
c_{1}
may affects the marginals of the variables in
c_{2}
and whether these variables are considered by the coupling process or not. However, we show that, as long as
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
do not share good variables, these dependencies are not very strong and we can indeed bound the probability that
c_{1},c_{2}\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{d}}(R)\cup\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{u}}(R)
with a careful probability argument that analyses the coupling process step by step, see Lemma
51
. With this in mind, we introduce the following definitions.

Definition 48

(
\mathcal{R}_{t}(\Phi,\mathcal{M},u,\Lambda)
,
\mathcal{A}_{\leq t}
)
.

For a positive integer
t
, we let
\mathcal{R}_{t}(\Phi,\mathcal{M},u,\Lambda)
be the set containing for each
R\in\mathcal{R}(\Phi,\mathcal{M},u,\Lambda)
a tuple with the first
\min\{t,\operatorname{length}(R)\}
entries of the sequence
R
. That is,
\mathcal{R}_{t}(\Phi,\mathcal{M},u,\Lambda)
is the set containing all possible sequences of the first
t
choices that Algorithm
4
makes in line
17
. Note that if
R\in\mathcal{R}(\Phi,\mathcal{M},u,\Lambda)
has
\operatorname{length}(R)\leq t
, then
R\in\mathcal{R}_{t}(\Phi,\mathcal{M},u,\Lambda)
. Each
R_{t}\in\mathcal{R}_{t}(\Phi,\mathcal{M},u,\Lambda)
determines two partial assignments
\Lambda^{\prime}
and
\Lambda^{\prime\prime}
of marked and auxiliary variables that correspond to the assignments
\widehat{X}
and
\widehat{Y}
after
\operatorname{length}(R_{t})
iterations of line
17
following
R_{t}
. Let
\mathcal{A}_{\leq t}
be the
\sigma
-algebra containing all the subsets of
\mathcal{R}_{t}(\Phi,\mathcal{M},u,\Lambda)
.

Intuitively,
\mathcal{A}_{\leq t}
contains all the possible events that may occur in the first
t
iterations of line
17
, which is the only randomised operation in Algorithm
4
. When bounding the probability that a clause is failed, we will express this event in terms of events concerning the values that
\widehat{X}
and
\widehat{Y}
take on its variables. This motivates Definition
49
.

Definition 49

(
D_{v}(j)
)
.

We define the following events
for variable
v\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{a}}
and a random run
R\sim\tau_{\mathcal{R}}(\Phi,\mathcal{M},u,\Lambda)
of Algorithm
4
. Let
D_{v}(1)
be the event that
v\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{set}}(R)
and
\widehat{X}(v)\neq\widehat{Y}(v)
. Let
D_{v}(2)
be the event that
v\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{set}}(R)
and
\widehat{X}(v)=\mathsf{F}
. Let
D_{v}(3)
be the event that
v\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{set}}(R)
and
\widehat{X}(v)=\mathsf{T}
. Let
D_{v}(4)
be the event that
v\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{set}}(R)
and
\widehat{Y}(v)=\mathsf{F}
. Let
D_{v}(5)
be the event that
v\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{set}}(R)
and
\widehat{Y}(v)=\mathsf{T}
.

Finally, in order to study the events
D_{v}(j)
for
v\in V
we will have to reason about the first time that a variable in
V
is added to
\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{set}}(R)
, which motivates the following definition.

Definition 50

(
\tau(V)
,
f(V)
)
.

For a set of auxiliary variables
V
, we let
\tau(V)
be the random variable that takes the value
t
if the first time that a variable in
V
is added to
\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{set}}(R)
in Algorithm
4
is the
t
-th time line
17
is executed, and we denote by
f(V)
this variable. We set
\tau(V)=\infty
if
V\cap\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{set}}(R)=\emptyset
, in which case
f(V)
is not defined.

We now have all the tools that we need to analyse the coupling process step by step.

Lemma 51
.

Let
V\subseteq\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{a}}
and let
i_{v}\in\{1,2,3,4,5\}
for each
v\in V
. Let
h(1)=2^{-r_{0}k+1}/k
and
h(i)=\frac{\exp(1/k)}{2}
for
i\in\{2,3,4,5\}
. Then, we have

\operatorname{Pr}_{R\sim\tau_{\mathcal{R}}(\Phi,\mathcal{M},u,\Lambda)}\left(\bigcap\nolimits_{v\in V}D_{v}(i_{v})\right)\leq\prod_{v\in V}h(i_{v}).

Proof.

We are going to prove, for any positive integer
t
and
A\in\mathcal{A}_{\leq t}
,

\operatorname{Pr}\left(\left.\bigcap\nolimits_{v\in V}D_{v}({i_{v}})\right|A,\tau(V)=t\right)\leq\prod_{v\in V}h(i_{v}).

(29)

The lemma will then follow from the arbitrary choice of
A
and
t
and the law of total probability.

We carry out the proof of (
29
) by induction on
M=\lvert V\rvert
.
Equation (
29
) holds when
V
is empty. Let us assume that (
29
) holds when
\lvert V\rvert<M
. Let
V
be a set of auxiliary variables with
M=\lvert V\rvert
and indexes
i_{v}
for all
v\in V
, let
t
be a positive integer and let
A\in\mathcal{A}_{\leq t}
. To simplify the notation,
for each
w\in V
we define
A_{t}(w,V)=A\cap[\tau(V)=t]\cap[f(V)=w]
.
Then, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{Pr}\left(\left.\bigcap\nolimits_{v\in V}D_{v}({i_{v}})\right|A,\tau(V)=t\right)\leq\sum_{w\in V}

\displaystyle\operatorname{Pr}\left(f(V)=w|A,\tau(V)=t\right)\cdot\operatorname{Pr}\left(\left.D_{w}({i_{w}})\right|A_{t}(w,V)\right)

\displaystyle\cdot\operatorname{Pr}\left(\left.\bigcap\nolimits_{v\in V\setminus\{w\}}D_{v}({i_{v}})\right|A_{t}(w,V),D_{w}({i_{w}})\right).

We note that
\tau(V\setminus\{w\})>t
when conditioning on
\tau(V)=t
and
f(V)=w
. Let
A^{\prime}=A_{t}(w,V)\cap D_{w}({i_{w}})
. We have

\displaystyle\operatorname{Pr}\left(\left.\bigcap\nolimits_{v\in V\setminus\{w\}}D_{v}({i_{v}})\right|A^{\prime}\right)=\sum_{j=t+1}^{\infty}

\displaystyle\operatorname{Pr}\left(\left.\tau(V\setminus\{w\})=j\right|A^{\prime}\right)

\displaystyle\cdot\operatorname{Pr}\left(\left.\bigcap\nolimits_{v\in V\setminus\{w\}}D_{v}({i_{v}})\right|A^{\prime},\tau(V\setminus\{w\})=j\right).

By our induction hypothesis for
V\setminus\{w\}
, the condition
\tau(V\setminus\{w\})=j
and the event
A^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{\leq j}
, we find that

\displaystyle\operatorname{Pr}\left(\left.\bigcap\nolimits_{v\in V\setminus\{w\}}D_{v}({i_{v}})\right|A^{\prime}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=t+1}^{\infty}\operatorname{Pr}\left(\left.\tau(V\setminus\{w\})=j\right|A^{\prime}\right)\prod_{v\in V\setminus\{w\}}h(i_{v})\leq\prod_{v\in V\setminus\{w\}}h(i_{v}).

As a consequence, we obtain

\displaystyle\operatorname{Pr}\left(\left.\bigcap\nolimits_{v\in V}D_{v}({i_{v}})\right|A,\tau(V)=t\right)\leq\sum_{w\in V}

\displaystyle\operatorname{Pr}\left(f(V)=w|A,\tau(V)=t\right)\cdot\operatorname{Pr}\left(\left.D_{w}({i_{w}})\right|A_{t}(w,V)\right)

\displaystyle\cdot\prod_{v\in V\setminus\{w\}}h(i_{v}).

We are going to show that
\operatorname{Pr}(D_{w}({i_{w}})|A_{t}(w,V))\leq h(i_{w})
.
Once we have proved this, the proof of (
29
) is completed by noting that
\sum_{w\in V}\operatorname{Pr}\left(f(V)=w|A,\tau(V)=t\right)=1
.

Let us now bound
\operatorname{Pr}(D_{w}(i_{w})|A_{t}(w,V))
. Recall here that
A_{t}(w,V)
implies the event
w\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{set}}(R)
. Recall also that
A_{t}(w,V)\in\mathcal{A}_{\leq t}
, see Definition
48
. For each
R_{t}\in A_{t}(w,V)\subseteq\mathcal{R}_{t}(\Phi,\mathcal{M},u,\Lambda)
, we are going to apply Proposition
10
and the fact that
\widehat{X}(w)
and
\widehat{Y}(w)
follow the optimal coupling between two marginal distributions on
w
of the form
\mu_{\Omega^{\Lambda^{\prime}}}
and
\mu_{\Omega^{\Lambda^{\prime\prime}}}
for some assignments
\Lambda^{\prime},\Lambda^{\prime\prime}
on some marked and auxiliary variables that are determined by
R_{t}
. Here it is important for applying Proposition
10
that the event
A_{t}(w,V)
is in
\mathcal{A}_{\leq t}
, so every partial run
R_{t}\in A_{t}(w,V)
only gives information about what has happened in Algorithm
4
before
w
is added to
\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{set}}(R)
. Thus, aggregating over all possible runs
R_{t}\in A_{t}(w,V)
, we find that

\displaystyle\max\left\{\operatorname{Pr}\left(\left.\widehat{X}(w)=\mathsf{F}\right|A_{t}(w,V)\right),\operatorname{Pr}\left(\left.\widehat{X}(w)=\mathsf{T}\right|A_{t}(w,V)\right)\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\exp\left(\frac{1}{k2^{r_{0}k}}\right)

(30)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\exp\big(\frac{1}{k}\big),

where the probability is over the random run
R\sim\tau_{\mathcal{R}}(\Phi,\mathcal{M},u,\Lambda)
. The bound (
30
) also applies with
\widehat{Y}
instead of
\widehat{X}
. In particular, we conclude that
\operatorname{Pr}(D_{w}(j)|A_{t}(w,V))\leq\exp(1/k)/2=h(j)
for all
j\in\{2,3,4,5\}
. Moreover, using the definition of optimal coupling for two Bernoulli distributions, the probability that
\widehat{X}(w)\neq\widehat{Y}(w)
can be bounded as

\displaystyle\operatorname{Pr}\left(\left.\widehat{X}(w)\neq\widehat{Y}(w)\right|A_{t}(w,V)\right)

\displaystyle=\left|\operatorname{Pr}\left(\left.\widehat{X}(w)=\mathsf{T}\right|A_{t}(w,V)\right)-\operatorname{Pr}\left(\left.\widehat{Y}(w)=\mathsf{T}\right|A_{t}(w,V)\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\operatorname{Pr}\left(\left.\widehat{X}(w)=\mathsf{T}\right|A_{t}(w,V)\right)-1/2\right|+\left|1/2-\operatorname{Pr}\left(\left.\widehat{Y}(w)=\mathsf{T}\right|A_{t}(w,V)\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(\frac{1}{k2^{r_{0}k}}\right)-1.

Hence, applying the bound
e^{z}\leq 1+2z
for
z\in(0,1)
and the definition of the event
D_{v_{j}}(1)
, we have

\operatorname{Pr}(D_{v_{j}}(1)|A_{t}(w,V))\leq 2/(k2^{r_{0}k})=h(1)
. This finishes the proof of (
29
). From the arbitrary choice of
A
and
t
and the law of total probability, the statement follows.
∎

We can now bound the probability that some good clauses are failed with the help of Lemma
51
.

Lemma 52
.

Let
\Phi,u,\Lambda
be the input of Algorithm
4
. Let
c_{1},\ldots,c_{\ell}\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{good}}
such that the variable
u
does not appear in any of the clauses in
c_{1},\ldots,c_{\ell}
, and
\operatorname{var}(c_{i})\cap\operatorname{var}(c_{j})\cap\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{good}}=\emptyset
for all
1\leq i<j\leq\ell
. Then, for
R\sim\tau_{\mathcal{R}}(\Phi,\mathcal{M},u,\Lambda)
, we have
\operatorname{Pr}(c_{1},\ldots,c_{\ell}\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{d}}(R)\cup\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{u}}(R))\leq 2^{(-r_{0}k+4)\ell}
.

Proof.

Let
c_{1},\ldots,c_{\ell}
be some good clauses of
\Phi
as in the statement. The hypothesis that
u
does not appear in any of these clauses is necessary as if
u\in\operatorname{var}(c)
then
c\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{d}}(R)
by definition.
We consider a random run
R\sim\tau_{\mathcal{R}}(\Phi,\mathcal{M},u,\Lambda)
of Algorithm
4
and let
\widehat{X},\widehat{Y}
be the (random) output of Algorithm
4
for the run
R
. For
j\in\{1,2,\ldots,\ell\}
, let
F_{j}(1)
be the event that there is
v\in\operatorname{var}(c_{j})\cap\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{a}}
such that
v\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{set}}(R)
and
\widehat{X}(v)\neq\widehat{Y}(v)
, let
F_{j}(2)
be the event that
\operatorname{var}(c_{j})\cap\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{a}}\subseteq\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{set}}(R)
and
c_{j}
is unsatisfied by
\widehat{X}
, and let
F_{j}(3)
be the event that
\operatorname{var}(c_{j})\cap\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{a}}\subseteq\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{set}}(R)
and
c_{j}
is unsatisfied by
\widehat{Y}
. In light of Proposition
41
, we have
[c_{1},\ldots,c_{\ell}\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{d}}(R)\cup\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{u}}(R)]=\bigcap_{j=1}^{\ell}(F_{j}(1)\cup F_{j}(2)\cup F_{j}(3))
. We obtain

\operatorname{Pr}\left(\bigcap\nolimits_{j=1}^{\ell}(F_{j}(1)\cup F_{j}(2)\cup F_{j}(3))\right)\leq\sum_{(i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{\ell})\in\{1,2,3\}^{\ell}}\operatorname{Pr}\left(\bigcap\nolimits_{j=1}^{\ell}F_{j}(i_{j})\right).

(31)

We note that
F_{j}(1)=\bigcup_{v\in\operatorname{var}(c_{j})\cap\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{a}}}D_{v}(1)
, see Definition
49
. Let
(i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{\ell})\in\{1,2,3\}^{\ell}
, and let
I_{1}=\{j:i_{j}=1\}
,
I_{2}=\{j:i_{j}=2\}
and
I_{3}=\{j:i_{j}=3\}
. If the event
\bigcap_{j\in I_{1}}F_{j}(i_{j})
holds, then, for each
j\in I_{1}
there is a variable
u_{j}\in\operatorname{var}(c_{j})\cap\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{a}}
such that
D_{u_{j}}(1)
holds. Thus, for the set of tuples
T=\prod_{j\in I_{1}}(\operatorname{var}(c_{j})\cap\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{a}})
, where
\prod
here denotes the cartesian product of sets, we have

\bigcap_{j\in I_{1}}F_{j}(i_{j})=\bigcup_{(u_{1},u_{2},\ldots,u_{|I_{1}|})\in T}\bigcap_{j\in I_{1}}D_{u_{j}}(1).

(32)

Now we explain how we bound
\operatorname{Pr}\left(\left(\bigcap_{j\in I_{2}\cup I_{3}}F_{j}(i_{j})\right)\cap\left(\bigcap_{j\in I_{1}}D_{u_{j}}(1)\right)\right)
for a tuple
(u_{1},u_{2},\ldots,u_{|I_{1}|})\in T
. We are going to show that

\operatorname{Pr}\left(\left(\bigcap\nolimits_{j\in I_{2}\cup I_{3}}F_{j}(i_{j})\right)\cap\left(\bigcap\nolimits_{j\in I_{1}}D_{u_{j}}(1)\right)\right)\leq\left(\frac{\exp(1/k)}{2}\right)^{(k-3)r_{0}\lvert I_{2}\cup I_{3}\rvert}\left(\frac{2}{k2^{r_{0}k}}\right)^{\lvert I_{1}\rvert}.

(33)

The proof of (
33
) is not as straightforward as it may seem at first glance due to the dependencies among the events
F_{j}(i_{j})
,
D_{u_{j}}(1)
. The key idea is re-writing the LHS of (
33
) as in the statement of Lemma
51
. Indeed we note that for each
j\in I_{2}
and for each variable
v\in\operatorname{var}(c_{j})\cap\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{a}}
, the event
F_{j}(2)
implies that there is
i_{v}\in\{2,3\}
such that
D_{v}(i_{v})
holds, concluding
F_{j}(2)=\bigcap_{v\in\operatorname{var}(c_{j})\cap\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{a}}}D_{v}(i_{v})
, see Definition
49
. Analogously, for each
j\in I_{3}
and for each variable
v\in\operatorname{var}(c_{j})\cap\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{a}}
, we find
i_{v}\in\{4,5\}
such that
F_{j}(3)=\bigcap_{v\in\operatorname{var}(c_{j})\cap\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{a}}}D_{v}(i_{v})
. Therefore, we have

\left(\bigcap\nolimits_{j\in I_{2}\cup I_{3}}F_{j}(i_{j})\right)\cap\left(\bigcap\nolimits_{j\in I_{1}}D_{u_{j}}(1)\right)=\bigcap_{v\in V_{f}}D_{v}({i_{v}}),

(34)

where
V_{f}
contains exactly all the auxiliary variables in the clauses
c_{j}
with
j\in I_{2}\cup I_{3}
and the variables
u_{1},u_{2},\ldots,u_{|I_{1}|}
. Recall now that each good clause contains at least
r_{0}(k-3)
auxiliary variables, and, thus, the bound given in (
33
) follows from (
34
) and Lemma
51
. Combining (
33
), (
32
) and (
31
), and counting the number of tuples in
T
, we conclude that

\displaystyle\operatorname{Pr}\left(\bigcap\nolimits_{j=1}^{\ell}(F_{j}(1)\cup F_{j}(2)\cup F_{j}(3))\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{(i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{\ell})\in\{1,2,3\}^{\ell}}k^{\lvert I_{1}\rvert}\left(\frac{\exp(1/k)}{2}\right)^{(k-3)r_{0}\lvert I_{2}\cup I_{3}\rvert}\left(\frac{2}{k2^{r_{0}k}}\right)^{\lvert I_{1}\rvert}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{(i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{\ell})\in\{1,2,3\}^{\ell}}\left(\frac{e2^{3r_{0}}}{2^{kr_{0}}}\right)^{\lvert I_{2}\cup I_{3}\rvert}\left(\frac{2}{2^{r_{0}k}}\right)^{\lvert I_{1}\rvert}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{e2^{3r_{0}}}{2^{kr_{0}}}+\frac{e2^{3r_{0}}}{2^{kr_{0}}}+\frac{2}{2^{r_{0}k}}\right)^{\ell},

where we used the multinomial theorem. The result now follows from
2e2^{3r_{0}}+2\leq 2^{4}
.
∎

Following
[
24
]
and motivated by Lemma
52
, we introduce the combinatorial structure that we use in our proof of Lemma
14
to bound the expected number of failed clauses.

Definition 53

(
G^{\leq k}
,
\mathcal{D}_{3}(G_{\Phi},c,\ell)
)
.

For a graph
G=(V,E)
and a positive integer
k
, let
G^{\leq k}
be the graph with vertex set
V
in which vertices
u
and
v
are connected if and only if there is a path from
u
to
v
in
G
of length at most
k
. Given the graph
G_{\Phi}
, a clause
c
and a positive integer
\ell
, let
\mathcal{D}_{3}(G_{\Phi},c,\ell)
be the set of subsets
T\subseteq V(G_{\Phi})
such that the following holds:

1.

\lvert T\rvert=\ell
and
c\in T
;

2.

for any
c_{1},c_{2}\in T
,
\operatorname{var}(c_{1})\cap\operatorname{var}(c_{2})\cap\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{good}}=\emptyset
;

3.

the graph
G_{\Phi}^{\leq 3}[T]
, which is the subgraph of
G_{\Phi}^{\leq 3}
induced by
T
, is connected;

4.

we have
\lvert T\cap\mathcal{C}_{good}\rvert\geq(1-8/k)\ell
.

In
[
24
]
the authors consider connected sets in
G_{\Phi}^{\leq 4}
instead of
G_{\Phi}^{\leq 3}
. Here we manage to perform our union bound on
\mathcal{D}_{3}(G_{\Phi},c,\ell)
thanks to the fact that the set of failed clauses is connected in our refinement of the coupling process.

Lemma 54

(
[
24
, Corollary 8.19]
for
G^{\leq 3}
)
.

Let
G=(V,E)
be a connected graph, let
v\in V
and let
\ell
be a positive integer. Let
n_{G,\ell}(v)
denote the number of connected induced subgraphs of
G
with size
\ell
containing
v
. Then, for
\ell^{\prime}=\min\{3\ell,\lvert V\rvert\}
, we have
n_{G^{\leq 3},\ell}(v)\leq 2^{\ell^{\prime}}n_{G,\ell^{\prime}}(v)
.

Proof.

Let
T
be a connected subgraph of
G^{\leq 3}
with size
\ell
containing
v
. We claim that, for all positive
\ell
, we can find a connected subset
H
of
G
with size
\ell^{\prime}=\min\{3\ell,\lvert V\rvert\}
containing
T
. The proof is straightforward by induction on
\ell
, see
[
24
, Lemma 8.18]
for the analogous result on
G^{\leq 4}
. We note that there are at most
\binom{\ell^{\prime}}{\ell-1}\leq 2^{\ell^{\prime}}
subsets
T
of
H
containing
v
that could be mapped to
H
by the previous construction. Hence, we conclude that
n_{G^{\leq 3},\ell}(v)\leq 2^{\ell^{\prime}}n_{G,\ell^{\prime}}(v)
as we wanted.
∎

Lemma 55

(
[
24
, Lemma 7.9]
for
\mathcal{D}_{3}(G_{\Phi},c,\ell)
)
.

Let
\ell
be an integer which is at least
\log n
. W.h.p. over the choice of
\Phi
, every clause
c\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{good}}
has the property that the size of
\mathcal{D}_{3}(G_{\Phi},c,\ell)
is at most
(18k^{2}\alpha)^{3\ell}
.

Proof.

This follows from bounding the number of connected sets of size
\ell
in
G_{\Phi}^{\leq 3}
that contain
c
by combining Lemmas
34
and
54
.
∎

We have now all the tools that we need to bound the expected number of failed clauses in the coupling process given in Algorithm
4
and complete the proof of Lemma
14
.

Lemma
14
.

There is an integer
k_{0}\geq 3
such that for any integer
k\geq k_{0}
and any density
\alpha
with
\alpha\leq 2^{r_{0}k/3}/k^{3}
the following holds. W.h.p. over the choice of the random
k
-CNF formula
\Phi=\Phi(k,n,\lfloor\alpha n\rfloor)
, for any
(r_{0}-\delta,r_{0},r_{0},2r_{0})
-marking
(\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}},\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{a}},\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{c}})
of
\Phi
, the distribution
{\left.\kern-1.2pt\mu_{\Omega}\right|_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}}}
is
(2^{-(r_{0}-\delta)k}\log n)
-spectrally independent.

Proof.

Let
u\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}
and
\Lambda\colon S\to\{\mathsf{F},\mathsf{T}\}
with
S\subseteq\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}\setminus\{u\}
. First of all, we apply Lemma
47
to bound
\sum_{v\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}\setminus(S\cup\{u\})}\left|\mathcal{I}^{\Lambda}(u\to v)\right|
by
2^{-r_{0}k+1}\mathbb{E}\left[\lvert\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{u}}(R)\rvert\right]
, where
R\sim\tau_{\mathcal{R}}(\Phi,\mathcal{M},u,\Lambda)
. In the rest of this proof we show that
\operatorname{Pr}(\lvert\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{u}}(R)\rvert\geq 2k^{4}\log n)\leq O(1/n)
and, thus, for large enough
n
,
\mathbb{E}\left[\lvert\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{u}}(R)\rvert\right]=\sum_{R\in\mathcal{R}(\Phi,\mathcal{M},u,\Lambda)}\operatorname{Pr}(R)\lvert\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{u}}(R)\rvert\leq 4k^{4}\log n
. Putting all this together, and using the fact that
8k^{4}\leq 2^{\delta k}
for large enough
k
(here
\delta=0.00001
) we would obtain the bound
\sum_{v\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}\setminus(S\cup\{u\})}\left|\mathcal{I}^{\Lambda}(u\to v)\right|\leq 8\cdot 2^{-r_{0}k}k^{4}\log n\leq 2^{-(r_{0}-\delta)k}\log n
and, thus, the result would follow.

So to finish we just need to show that, w.h.p. over the choice of
\Phi
,
\operatorname{Pr}(\lvert\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{u}}(R)\rvert\geq 2k^{4}\log n)\leq O(1/n)
. Let
L=\lceil 2k^{4}\log n\rceil
and let
\ell=\lceil 0.5k^{4}\log n\rceil
. First, we are going to show that, w.h.p. over the choice of
\Phi
, the following holds:

\displaystyle\text{if }Z\subseteq\mathcal{C}\text{ is connected and }\lvert Z\rvert=L

\displaystyle\text{, then }\exists c\in Z\cap\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{good}}\text{ and }T\in\mathcal{D}_{3}(G_{\Phi},c,\ell)\text{ with }T\subseteq Z.

(35)

In order to prove (
35
), we are going to find a large independent set of
Z\cap\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{good}}
, and we are going to extend it with some clauses in
Z\cap\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{bad}}
to obtain
T\in\mathcal{D}_{3}(G_{\Phi},c,\ell)
. We need three results that hold w.h.p. over the choice of
\Phi
: Lemmas
20
,
32
and
31
. We note that we can apply Lemma
20
for
r=r_{0}-\delta
as our density satisfies
\alpha\leq 2^{r_{0}k/3}/k^{3}\leq\lceil 2^{(r_{0}-\delta)k}\rceil/k^{3}=\Delta_{r}/k^{3}
, where
\delta=0.00001
. For
Z
as in (
35
) we have
\lvert Z\rvert\geq 2k^{4}\log n
, so by Lemma
32
with
a=2k^{4}
, we find that
\lvert\operatorname{var}(Z)\rvert\geq 2k^{4}\log n
and, thus, in light of Lemma
20
, we conclude that
\lvert Z\cap\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{good}}\rvert\geq(1-1/k)\lvert Z\rvert
and
\lvert Z\cap\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{bad}}\rvert\leq\lvert Z\rvert/k
. From Lemma
31
with
b=4k^{4}
, w.h.p. over the choice of
\Phi
, all connected sets of clauses with size at most
4k^{4}\log n
have tree-excess at most
t:=\max\{1,8k^{4}\log(ek^{2}\alpha)\}
.
Thus, we can find
U\subseteq Z\cap\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{good}}
such that
U
is a forest (disjoint union of trees) and
\lvert U\rvert\geq(1-1/k)\lvert Z\rvert-t
. In particular,
U
is bipartite, so there is
I\subseteq U
such that
\operatorname{var}(c)\cap\operatorname{var}(c^{\prime})=\emptyset
for all
c,c^{\prime}\in I
and
\lvert I\rvert\geq\lvert U\rvert/2\geq(1-1/k)L/2-t/2\geq\tfrac{1}{2}k^{4}\log n
, where the last inequality holds for large enough
n
. Let
I^{\prime}
be an independent set of
Z\cap\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{good}}
with the largest possible size. Then we have shown that
\lvert I^{\prime}\rvert\geq\ell=\lceil\tfrac{1}{2}k^{4}\log n\rceil
.

We claim that the set
T^{\prime}:=I^{\prime}\cup(Z\cap\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{bad}})
is connected in
(G_{\Phi}[Z])^{\leq 3}
, where
G_{\Phi}[Z]
is the subgraph of
G_{\Phi}
induced by
Z
. Assume for contradiction that
T^{\prime}
is not connected in
(G_{\Phi}[Z])^{\leq 3}
. In this case, we can write
T^{\prime}=S_{1}\cup S_{2}
such that for all
c_{1}\in S_{1}
and
c_{2}\in S_{2}
, the shortest path between
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
through clauses in
Z
has length at least
4
. Let
(c_{1},c_{2})\in S_{1}\times S_{2}
be the pair with the shortest path in
Z
, and let this path be
c_{1}=e_{1},e_{2},\ldots,e_{j}=c_{2}
. Then
j\geq 5
and
e_{2},\ldots,e_{j-1}\in Z\setminus T^{\prime}
. Moreover, we find that
\operatorname{var}(e_{3})\cap\operatorname{var}(c)=\emptyset
for all
c\in T^{\prime}
as otherwise
e_{1},e_{2},\ldots,e_{j}
would not be the shortest path between
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
. Moreover, since
T^{\prime}
contain all bad clauses in
Z
, we conclude that
e_{3}
is a good clause.
It follows that
I^{\prime}\cup\{e_{3}\}
is an independent set of good clauses of
Z
, which contradicts the fact that
I^{\prime}
has largest possible size among such sets.

Finally, as
\lvert T^{\prime}\rvert\geq\ell
, we can find a good clause
c
and a subset
T
of
T^{\prime}
with size
\ell
such that
c\in T
,
T
is connected in
G_{\Phi}^{\leq 3}
and
\lvert T\cap\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{bad}}\rvert\leq\lvert Z\cap\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{bad}}\rvert\leq L/k\leq 8\ell/k
. We conclude that
T\in\mathcal{D}_{3}(G_{\Phi},c,\ell)
. This finishes the proof of (
35
).

In the rest of the proof we use (
35
) to bound
\operatorname{Pr}(\lvert\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{u}}(R)\rvert\geq L)
. Recall that the set of failed clauses
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{d}}(R)\cup\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{u}}(R)
is connected (Proposition
41
). If
\lvert\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{u}}(R)\rvert\geq L
, then there is
Z\subseteq\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{d}}(R)\cup\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{u}}(R)
with
\lvert Z\rvert=L
such that
Z
is connected in
G_{\Phi}
, and, thus, we can find
c
and
T
as in (
35
). We have shown that the event
\lvert\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{u}}(R)\rvert\geq L
is contained in the event that there is a good clause
c
and
T\in\mathcal{D}_{3}(\Phi,c,\ell)
such that
T\subseteq\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{d}}(R)\cup\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{u}}(R)
. As a consequence,
we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{Pr}\left[\lvert\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{u}}(R)\rvert\geq L\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{c\in\mathcal{C}}\ \sum_{T\in\mathcal{D}_{3}(\Phi,c,\ell)}\operatorname{Pr}\left[T\subseteq\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{d}}(R)\cup\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{u}}(R)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{c\in\mathcal{C}}\ \sum_{T\in\mathcal{D}_{3}(\Phi,c,\ell)}\operatorname{Pr}\left[T\cap\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{good}}\subseteq\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{d}}(R)\cup\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{u}}(R)\right].

We note that for any
T\in\mathcal{D}_{3}(\Phi,c,\ell)
there is at most one good clause
c^{\prime}
that contains the marked variable
u
. Thus, by definition of
\mathcal{D}_{3}(\Phi,c,\ell)
, there are at least
(1-8/k)\ell-1
good clauses in
T
that do not contain the variable
u
.
Hence, we can apply Lemma
55
on the size of
\mathcal{D}_{3}(\Phi,c,\ell)
and Lemma
52
on the probability of good clauses (that do not share good variables) failing to further obtain

\displaystyle\operatorname{Pr}\left[\lvert\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{u}}(R)\rvert\geq L\right]

\displaystyle\leq m\left(18k^{2}\alpha\right)^{3\ell}2^{-(r_{0}k-4)[(1-8/k)\ell-1]}.

In what follows it is essential that
\alpha\leq 2^{r_{0}k/3}/k^{3}
, and this is the only proof in this paper where we need this bound on the density – other proofs only require the less restrictive bounds
\alpha\leq 2^{(r_{0}-\delta)k}/k^{3}
or
\alpha\leq 2^{(r_{0}-3\delta)k}/k^{3}
. Thus, we conclude that

\displaystyle\operatorname{Pr}\left[\lvert\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{u}}(R)\rvert\geq L\right]

\displaystyle\leq m\left(18\frac{2^{r_{0}k/3}}{k}\right)^{3\ell}2^{-(r_{0}k-4)(1-8/k)\ell}\ 2^{r_{0}k-4}=m\left(\frac{18^{3}}{k^{3}}2^{8r_{0}+4(1-8/k)}\right)^{\ell}2^{r_{0}k-4}.

Finally, for large enough
k
we find that
\operatorname{Pr}\left[\lvert\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{u}}(R)\rvert\geq L\right]\leq me^{-\ell}2^{r_{0}k}\leq mn^{-0.5k^{4}}2^{r_{0}k}=O(1/n)
as we wanted.
∎

8.3
Mixing time of the
\rho
-uniform-block Glauber dynamics

Finally, we combine the results in this section with Lemma
13
to complete the proof of Lemma
15
.

Remark 56
.

The distribution
{\left.\kern-1.2pt\mu_{\Omega}\right|_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}}}
on assignments of the marked variables (Definition
11
) is
b
-marginally bounded for
b=1-(1/2)\exp(1/k)
by Proposition
10
(or, equivalently, Lemmas
26
and
28
). Since
\exp(1/k)\leq 1+2/k
, we have
b\geq 1/2-1/k\geq 1/e
for
k\geq 8
.

Lemma
15
.

There is a function
k_{0}(\theta)=\Theta(\log(1/\theta))
such that, for any
\theta\in(0,1)
, for any integer
k\geq k_{0}(\theta)
and any density
\alpha
with
\alpha\leq 2^{0.039k}
the following holds. W.h.p. over the choice of the random
k
-CNF formula
\Phi=\Phi(k,n,\lfloor\alpha n\rfloor)
, for any
(r_{0}-\delta,r_{0},r_{0},2r_{0})
-marking
(\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}},\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{a}},\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{c}})
of
\Phi
and for
\rho=\lceil 2^{-k-1}\lvert\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}\rvert\rceil
, the
\rho
-uniform-block Glauber dynamics for updating the marked variables has mixing time
T_{\mix}(\rho,\varepsilon/2)\leq T:=\lceil 2^{2k+3}n^{\theta}\log\frac{2n}{\varepsilon^{2}}\rceil
.

Proof.

In view of Lemma
14
, as
\alpha\leq 2^{0.039k}\leq 2^{r_{0}k/3}/k^{3}
for large enough
k
, w.h.p. over the choice of
\Phi
, the distribution
{\left.\kern-1.2pt\mu_{\Omega}\right|_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}}}
is
\eta
-spectrally independent for
\eta=2^{-(r_{0}-\delta)k}\log n
. Moreover, this distribution is
b
-marginally bounded for
b=1/e
when
k\geq 8
. We are going to apply Lemma
13
with
V=\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}
,
\mu={\left.\kern-1.2pt\mu_{\Omega}\right|_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}}}
,
M=\lvert\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}\rvert
and
\kappa=2^{-k-1}
. First, we check that the hypothesis
M\geq\frac{2}{\kappa}(4\eta/b^{2}+1)
of Lemma
13
holds. By Corollary
30
with
r=r_{0}-\delta
and
V=\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}
, we have
M\geq(r_{0}-\delta)(k\alpha/\Delta_{r})n=\Omega(n)
, so
M\geq\frac{2}{\kappa}(4\eta/b^{2}+1)
holds for large enough
n
as
\frac{2}{\kappa}(4\eta/b^{2}+1)=O(\log n)
. Hence, we can apply Lemma
13
to obtain

\displaystyle T_{\mix}(\rho,\varepsilon/2)

\displaystyle\leq\left\lceil C_{\rho}\frac{M}{\rho}\left(\log\log\frac{1}{\mu_{\min}}+\log\frac{2}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right)\right\rceil,

where
\rho=\lceil\kappa M\rceil
and
C_{\rho}=\left(2/\kappa\right)^{4\eta/b^{2}+1}
. We have

C_{\rho}=\exp\left((\log 2)(k+2)\Big(\frac{4\eta}{b^{2}}+1\Big)\right)\leq 2^{k+2}\exp\left(\frac{(\log 2)(\log n)(k+2)4e^{2}}{2^{(r_{0}-\delta)k}}\right),

so there exists a function
k_{0}(\theta)=\Theta(\log(1/\theta))
such that when
k\geq k_{0}(\theta)
, we have
C_{\rho}\leq 2^{k+2}n^{\theta}
. In light of Remark
56
, we have
\mu_{\min}\geq b^{M}
, so
\log\log(1/\mu_{\min})\leq\log(M\log(1/b))=\log M
as
b=1/e
. Thus, we conclude that

T_{\mix}(\rho,\varepsilon/2)\leq\left\lceil 2^{2k+3}n^{\theta}\left(\log M+\log\frac{2}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right)\right\rceil\leq\left\lceil 2^{2k+3}n^{\theta}\log\frac{2n}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right\rceil.\qed

9
Proof of Theorem
1

In this section we complete the proof of Theorem
1
. The proofs in this section do not present any challenging steps. In fact, they amount to combining the main technical results that have already been proved in this work. We start by showing that the calls to the method
\operatorname{Sample}
in Algorithm
1
are unlikely to ever return error, that is, the connected components of
G_{\Phi^{\Lambda}}
have size at most
2k^{4}(1+\xi)\log(n)
almost every time the method is called. As pointed out in our proof outline, this is a straightforward consequence of Lemma
17
and the fact that the probability distribution of the output of the Glauber dynamics is
(1/k)
-uniform (Corollary
29
).

Lemma 57
.

Let
\theta\in(0,1)
. There is an integer
k_{0}\geq 3
such that, for any integers
k\geq k_{0}
,
\xi\geq 1
and any density
\alpha\leq 2^{(r_{0}-3\delta)k}/k^{3}
, the following holds w.h.p. over the choice of
\Phi=\Phi(k,n,\lfloor\alpha n\rfloor)
. In the execution of Algorithm
1
with input
\Phi
, with probability at least
1-n^{-3\xi}
over the random choices made by Algorithm
1
, every time that the algorithm calls the method
\operatorname{Sample}(\Phi^{\Lambda},S)
, the connected components of
G_{\Phi^{\Lambda}}
have size at most
2k^{4}(1+\xi)\log(n)
.

Proof.

Let
\varepsilon=n^{-\xi}
and let
T=\lceil 2^{2k+3}n^{\theta}\log\frac{2n}{\varepsilon^{2}}\rceil
be the mixing time established in Lemma
15
. Note that
\alpha\leq 2^{(r_{0}-3\delta)k}/k^{3}\leq 2^{(r_{0}-\delta)k}/k^{3}
, so we an indeed compute the marking
(\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}},\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{a}},\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{c}})
in Algorithm
1
with the help of Lemma
26
. We need
\alpha\leq 2^{(r_{0}-3\delta)k}/k^{3}
so that we can apply Lemma
17
with
r=r_{0}-\delta
.
Algorithm
1
calls the method
\operatorname{Sample}
exactly
T+1
times in total:
T
times in line
7
(when simulating the
\rho
-uniform-block Glauber dynamics) and one time in line
10
to extend the assignment
X_{T}
of marked variables to all variables.

Let
t\in\{0,1,\ldots,T\}
and let
\pi_{t}
be the probability distribution of
X_{t}
, where
X_{t}
is the state of the
\rho
-block-uniform Glauber dynamics on the marked variables described in Algorithm
1
after
t
steps. Recall that
\rho=\lceil 2^{-k-1}\lvert\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}\rvert\rceil
and that
X_{0}
is chosen uniformly at random. First, we focus on the case
t<T
. We are going to apply Lemma
17
with
r=r_{0}-\delta
,
a=2k^{4}
,
b=2a(1+\xi)
,
V=\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}
,
\mu=\pi_{t}
and this choice of
\rho
. The set
\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}
is
r_{0}
-distributed by the definition of
(r_{0}-\delta,r_{0},r_{0},2r_{0})
-marking (Definition
8
). Moreover,
\pi_{t}
is
(1/k)
-uniform by Corollary
29
, and we have
\rho\leq\lvert\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}\rvert/2^{k}
. Hence, we can indeed apply Lemma
17
. Consider the following experiment described in Lemma
17
for
L=\lceil a(1+\xi)\log n\rceil
, which satisfies
a\log n\leq L\leq b\log n
. First, draw
S\subseteq\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}
from the uniform distribution
\tau
over subsets of
\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}
with size
\rho
. Then, sample an assignment
\Lambda_{t+1}
from
{\left.\kern-1.2pt\pi_{t}\right|_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}\setminus S}}
, the marginal of
\pi_{t}
on
\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}\setminus S
. Denote by
\mathcal{F}
the event that that there is a connected set of clauses
Y
of
\Phi
with
\lvert Y\rvert\geq L
such that all clauses in
Y
are unsatisfied by
\Lambda_{t+1}
. Then we have

\operatorname{Pr}_{S\sim\tau}\left(\operatorname{Pr}_{\Lambda_{t+1}\sim{\left.\kern-1.2pt\pi_{t}\right|_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}\setminus S}}}\left(\mathcal{F}\right)\leq 2^{-\delta kL}\right)\geq 1-2^{-\delta kL}.

(36)

Alternatively, this experiment is the same as first sampling an assignment
X_{t}
of all variables in
\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}
from
\pi_{t}
, and then restricting the assignment to a random set
S\sim\tau
, obtaining
\Lambda_{t+1}
. Note that this exact experiment occurs before calling the method
\operatorname{Sample}
in the
t
-th step of the
\rho
-uniform-block Glauber dynamics in Algorithm
1
. Thus, in light of (
36
), the probability that in step
t+1
of the execution of Algorithm
1
the graph
G_{\Phi^{\Lambda_{t+1}}}
has a connected component with size at least
L
is at most
2^{-\delta kL}+2^{-\delta kL}
, where the first
2^{-\delta kL}
comes from the probability of choosing a wrong set
S\sim\tau
in (
36
) and the second
2^{-\delta kL}
comes from the bound on the probability of the event
\mathcal{F}
once we have chosen
S
. We have shown that with probability at least
1-{2\cdot}2^{-\delta kL}
,
all the connected components of the graph
G_{\Phi^{\Lambda_{t}}}
appearing in
step
t+1
of
the execution of Algorithm
1
have size less than
L
. We have
{2\cdot}2^{-\delta kL}\leq{2\cdot}n^{-\delta ka(1+\xi)\log 2}\leq n^{-5\xi}
for large enough
k
, so the probability that
\operatorname{Sample}
returns error at step
t+1
is at most
n^{-5\xi}
. The case
t=T
is analogous, the only difference here is that we call
\operatorname{Sample}
on
\Phi^{X_{T}}
, where
X_{T}\sim\pi_{T}
is an assignment of all marked variables, so we apply Lemma
17
with
\rho=0
instead of
\rho=\lceil 2^{-k-1}\lvert\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}\rvert\rceil
.

Finally, we carry out a union bound over
t\in\{0,1,\ldots,T\}
, so the probability that any of the calls to
\operatorname{Sample}
returns error is at most
(T+1)n^{-5\xi}\leq n^{-3\xi}
for large enough
n
as
T=O(n^{\theta}\log n)=O(n\log n)
.
∎

Once we have established Lemmas
15
,
19
, and
57
, the proof of Theorem
1
follows as below.

Theorem
1
.

For any real
\theta\in(0,1)
, there is
k_{0}\geq 3
with
k_{0}=O(\log(1/\theta))
such that, for any integers
k\geq k_{0}
and
\xi\geq 1
, and for any positive real
\alpha\leq 2^{0.039k}
, the following holds.

There is an efficient algorithm to sample from the satisfying assignments of a random
k
-CNF formula
\Phi=\Phi(k,n,\lfloor\alpha n\rfloor)
within
n^{-\xi}
total variation distance of the uniform distribution. The algorithm runs in time
O(n^{1+\theta})
, and succeeds w.h.p. over the choice of
\Phi
.

Proof.

Let
k_{0}(\theta)=\Theta(\log(1/\theta))
be large enough so that, for all integers
k\geq k_{0}(\theta)
,
\xi\geq 1
and all densities
\alpha\leq 2^{0.039\cdot k}
, the conclusions of
Lemmas
26
,
15
,
19
, and
57
hold w.h.p. over the choice of the random
k
-CNF formula
\Phi=\Phi(k,n,\lfloor\alpha n\rfloor)
. These lemmas are enough to analyse Algorithm
1
and tackle this proof. We analyse the distribution
\mu_{alg}
of the output of Algorithm
1
. This distribution outputs either a satisfying assignment of the input formula
\Phi
or
error
.
Let
\varepsilon=n^{-\xi}
. Let
\mathcal{E}
be the event that running Algorithm
1
outputs
error
. This happens with probability at most
\varepsilon/4
when computing the marking
(\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}},\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{a}},\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{c}})
in line 2 of the algorithm,
and in lines 7 and 10 if the method
\operatorname{Sample}(\hat{\Phi},S)
returns error, which occurs when
G_{\hat{\Phi}}
has a connected component with size more than
2k^{4}(1+\xi)\log(n)
. In view of Lemma
57
, the probability that Algorithm
1
outputs
error
due to the failure of the method
\operatorname{Sample}
is at most
n^{-3\xi}=\varepsilon^{3}
. We conclude that the probability that the algorithm outputs error is bounded above by
\varepsilon/4+\varepsilon^{3}\leq\varepsilon/2
.
Let
\mu_{Glauber}
be the distribution that Algorithm
1
would output if there were no errors
(that is, the distribution assuming that the method Sample always
outputs from the appropriate distribution). Then
d_{\TV}(\mu_{alg},\mu_{Glauber})

is the probability that an error occurs, which is
at most
\varepsilon/2
. Let
\pi_{Glauber}
be the distribution output by the
\rho
-uniform-block Glauber dynamics on
\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}
after
T
steps. By Lemma
15
on the mixing time of the Glauber dynamics, we have
d_{\TV}(\pi_{Glauber},{\left.\kern-1.2pt\mu_{\Omega}\right|_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}}})\leq\varepsilon/2
.
As
\mu_{Glauber}
comes from sampling an assignment
X_{T}
from
\pi_{Glauber}
and then completing
X_{T}
to all
\mathcal{V}
by sampling from
\mu_{\Omega}(\cdot|X_{T})
, we have
d_{\TV}(\mu_{Glauber},\mu_{\Omega})\leq d_{\TV}(\pi_{Glauber},{\left.\kern-1.2pt\mu_{\Omega}\right|_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}}})\leq\varepsilon/2
. We find that
d_{\TV}(\mu_{alg},\mu_{\Omega})\leq d_{\TV}(\mu_{alg},\mu_{Glauber})+d_{\TV}(\mu_{Glauber},\mu_{\Omega})\leq\varepsilon/2+\varepsilon/2=\varepsilon

as we wanted. The running time of Algorithm
1
is now easily obtained by adding up the running times of the following subroutines. The good clauses and good variables are computed in time
O(n+km)=O(n)
, see Proposition
7
. The marking
(\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}},\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{a}},\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{c}})
is computed with probability at least
1-\varepsilon/4
in time
O(n\Delta_{r}k^{2}\log(4/\varepsilon))=O(n\log n)
, see Lemma
26
. Recall that there are
T+1=O(n^{\theta}\log(n/\varepsilon^{2}))=O(n^{\theta}\log n)
calls to the method
\operatorname{Sample}(\Phi^{\prime},S)
, and each call takes time
O(|S\rvert\log n)=O(n\log n)
by Lemma
19
. We conclude that the running time of Algorithm
1
is
O(n^{1+\theta}\log(n)^{2})
. The result now follows by choosing
k_{1}=k_{0}(\theta/2)
, so the running time for
k\geq k_{1}
is
O(n^{1+\theta/2}\log(n)^{2})=O(n^{1+\theta})
.
∎

We have now proved that it is possible to (approximately) sample uniformly at random from the satisfying assignments of
\Phi=\Phi(k,n,\lfloor\alpha n\rfloor)
. At this point, standard techniques can be applied to obtain a randomised approximation scheme for counting the satisfying assignments of
\Phi
. These techniques are based on the self-reducibility of
k
-SAT
[
33
]
. The following remark shows how to obtain a randomised approximation scheme that runs in time
O(n^{\theta}(n/\varepsilon)^{2})
following
[
21
, Chapter 7]
, where the authors base their counting algorithm on the simulated annealing method
[
47
,
30
,
35
]
.

Remark 58

(Approximate counting for random
k
-SAT formulae)
.

Let
k_{0}(\theta)
be the integer depending on
\theta\in(0,1)
obtained in Theorem
1
. Let
k_{1}=k_{0}(\theta/2)
, let
k\geq k_{1}
be an integer, let
\xi
be a positive integer and let
\alpha\leq 2^{0.039k}
be a density. We apply Theorem
1
to obtain an algorithm to sample from the satisfying assignments of
\Phi=\Phi(k,n,\lfloor\alpha n\rfloor)
within
n^{-4\xi}
total variation distance from the uniform distribution. This algorithm runs in time
O(n^{1+\theta/2})
and succeeds w.h.p. over the choice of
\Phi
.

Let
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
with
\varepsilon\geq n^{-\xi}
. A modified version of the approximate counting algorithm of
[
21
, Section 7]
, using
O(\varepsilon^{-2}n\log(n/\varepsilon))
samples from the sampling algorithm above, approximates the number of satisfying assignments of the
k
-CNF formula
\Phi
with multiplicative error
\varepsilon
, thus, achieving running time
O(n^{\theta/2}(n/\varepsilon)^{2}\log(n/\varepsilon))=O(n^{\theta}(n/\varepsilon)^{2})
. Here we describe these minor modifications.

Let
\Omega_{\bad}
be the set of assignments
X\colon\mathcal{V}\to\{\mathsf{F},\mathsf{T}\}
that satisfy the bad clauses of
\Phi
. For
X\in\Omega_{\bad}
, we define
F(X)
to be the set of good clauses that are not satisfied by
X
. For
\kappa\in\mathbb{R}
, we define
w_{\kappa}(X)=\exp(-\kappa\lvert F(X)\rvert)
and we define the partition function
Z(\kappa)=\sum_{X\in\Omega_{\bad}}w_{\kappa}(X)
. The simulated annealing algorithm of
[
21
, Section 7]
uses
Z(\kappa)

(with
\Omega^{*}
from Definition
9
in place of
\Omega_{\bad}
)
to approximate the number of satisfying assignments of
\Phi
. We note that
Z(0)=\lvert\Omega_{\bad}\rvert
, which can be computed in linear time in
n
using the exact counting algorithm given in Proposition
36
.
Here one has to use the fact that the connected components of
G_{\Phi^{\prime}}
for the formula
\Phi^{\prime}=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{bad}})
have size at most
2k^{4}\log n
, see Lemma
71
from Appendix
A
and Lemma
32
, and the fact that these connected component have tree-excess upper bounded as a function of
k
(Lemma
31
). Once one has performed these modifications, the algorithm given in
[
21
, Section 7]
applies without any difficulties.

10
Proof of Theorems
3
and
5

In this section we exploit Lemma
17
to prove Theorems
3
and
5
on the connectivity and looseness of the solution space of random
k
-CNF formulae. We recall that the density threshold in Theorems
3
and
5
is
\alpha\leq 2^{0.227k}
, significantly larger than our algorithmic threshold in Theorem
1
, which is
\alpha\leq 2^{0.039k}
. In order to conclude connectivity for densities up to
2^{0.227k}
, we let
r_{1}=0.227092
and consider the threshold
\Delta_{r}=\lceil 2^{rk}\rceil
for
r=r_{1}-\delta
in the definition of high-degree variables instead of
\Delta_{r_{0}-\delta}=\lceil 2^{(r_{0}-\delta)k}\rceil
. In all this section we set
r=r_{1}-\delta
, so we omit
r
in the notation and we write
\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{good}}
instead of
\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{good}}(r)
. We work with an
(r,r_{1},0,r_{1})
-marking
(\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}},\emptyset,\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{c}})
(the set of auxiliary variables is empty), which we can find w.h.p. over the choice of
\Phi=\Phi(k,n,\lfloor\alpha n\rfloor)
as in Lemma
27
. Let us briefly recall some of the properties of this marking. First of all, by definition, the set
\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}
is
r_{1}
-distributed and is a subset of
\mathcal{V}_{good}
. Moreover, the distribution
{\left.\kern-1.2pt\mu_{\Omega}\right|_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}}}
is
(1/k)
-uniform by Lemma
28
. In light of Lemma
17
for
r=r_{1}-\delta
, these properties allow us to show that, when sampling
\Lambda\sim{\left.\kern-1.2pt\mu_{\Omega}\right|_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}}}
, the connected components of
\Phi^{\Lambda}
are logarithmic in size with probability
1-o(1)
over the choice
\Lambda\sim{\left.\kern-1.2pt\mu_{\Omega}\right|_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}}}
. In fact, this is also the case when
\Lambda\sim{\left.\kern-1.2pt\mu_{\Omega}\right|_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}\setminus\{v\}}}
for any variable
v
.

Corollary 59
.

There is an integer
k_{0}\geq 3
such that, for any integer
k\geq k_{0}
, any density
\alpha\leq\alpha_{1}:=2^{(r_{1}-3\delta)k}
, the following holds w.h.p. over the choice of
\Phi=\Phi(k,n,\lfloor\alpha n\rfloor)
.

Let
V
be a set of good variables of
\Phi
that is
r_{1}
-distributed, let
\mu
be a
(1/k)
-uniform distribution over the assignments
V\to\{\mathsf{F},\mathsf{T}\}
and let
v\in V
. Then, with probability at least
1-n^{-k}
over the choice
\Lambda\sim{\left.\kern-1.2pt\mu\right|_{V\setminus\{v\}}}
, the connected components of
\Phi^{\Lambda}
have size at most
2k^{4}\log n
.

Proof.

The result is an application of Lemma
17
with
r=r_{1}-\delta
,
b=4k^{4}
,
\rho=1
and
L=\lceil 2k^{4}\log n\rceil
. We need large enough
k_{0}
such that
2^{-\delta kL}\leq 2^{-\delta 2k^{5}\log n}\leq n^{-k}
for all
k\geq k_{0}
. For these parameters, in the setting of Lemma
17
, the distribution
\tau
is the uniform distribution over the variables in
V
. The experiment in the statement of Lemma
17
consists in sampling
v\sim\tau
and then sampling
\Lambda\sim{\left.\kern-1.2pt\mu\right|_{V\setminus\{v\}}}
. Let
\mathcal{F}_{v}
be the event, concerning the choice
\Lambda\sim{\left.\kern-1.2pt\mu\right|_{V\setminus\{v\}}}
, that there is a connected set of clauses
Y
of
\Phi
with
\lvert Y\rvert\geq\lceil 2k^{4}\log n\rceil
such that all clauses in
Y
are unsatisfied by
\Lambda
. Then by Lemma
17
we have
\operatorname{Pr}_{v\sim\tau}\left(\operatorname{Pr}_{\Lambda\sim{\left.\kern-1.2pt\mu\right|_{V\setminus\{v\}}}}\left(\mathcal{F}_{v}\right)\leq 2^{-\delta kL}\right)\geq 1-2^{-\delta kL}
. From
2^{-\delta kL}\leq n^{-k}
, we obtain the bound
\operatorname{Pr}_{v\sim\tau}\left(\operatorname{Pr}_{\Lambda\sim{\left.\kern-1.2pt\mu\right|_{V\setminus\{v\}}}}\left(\mathcal{F}_{v}\right)\leq 2^{-\delta kL}\right)\geq 1-n^{-k}
. Since
\tau
is the uniform distribution over the variables in
V
, for
v\sim\tau
, either the event that
\operatorname{Pr}_{\Lambda\sim{\left.\kern-1.2pt\mu\right|_{V\setminus\{v\}}}}\left(\mathcal{F}_{v}\right)\leq 2^{-\delta kL}
has probability
1
or it has probability at most
1-1/\lvert V\rvert\leq 1-1/n
. The latter option is not possible due to
\operatorname{Pr}_{v\sim\tau}\left(\operatorname{Pr}_{\Lambda\sim{\left.\kern-1.2pt\mu\right|_{V\setminus\{v\}}}}\left(\mathcal{F}_{v}\right)\leq 2^{-\delta kL}\right)\geq 1-n^{-k}
and
k\geq 3
. Thus, we conclude that
\operatorname{Pr}_{v\sim\tau}\left(\operatorname{Pr}_{\Lambda\sim{\left.\kern-1.2pt\mu\right|_{V\setminus\{v\}}}}\left(\mathcal{F}_{v}\right)\leq 2^{-\delta kL}\right)=1
, so for any
v\in V
we have
\operatorname{Pr}_{\Lambda\sim{\left.\kern-1.2pt\mu\right|_{V\setminus\{v\}}}}\left(\mathcal{F}_{v}\right)\leq 2^{-\delta kL}\leq n^{-k}
. That is, for any
v\in V
, with probability at least
1-n^{-k}
over the choice of
\Lambda\sim{\left.\kern-1.2pt\mu\right|_{V\setminus\{v\}}}
the connected components of
\Phi^{\Lambda}
have size at most
L-1=\lceil 2k^{4}\log n\rceil-1<2k^{4}\log n
as we wanted to prove.
∎

Our connectivity and looseness results will follow from Corollary
59
. In Section
10.1
we prove Theorem
3
and in Section
10.2
we prove Theorem
5
.

10.1
Proof of Theorem
3

We consider Algorithm
5
that receives two satisfying assignments of a
k
-CNF formula
\Phi
as the input and constructs a path between them. Before introducing this algorithm, recall that the graph
H_{\Phi}
is the dependency graph of the variables of
\Phi
introduced in Definition
18
.

0:

a
k
-CNF formula
\Phi=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{C})
with
n
variables, an
(r,r_{1},0,r_{1})
-marking
(\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}},\emptyset,\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{c}})
of
\Phi
, and two satisfying assignments
\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}
.

1:

Let
v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{\ell}
be the variables in
\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}
.

2:

\zeta_{0}\leftarrow\sigma
.

/*
Stage 1: Update the marked variables
*/

3:

for

i\in[\ell]

do

4:

Find
\zeta_{i}\in\Omega
with marked variables specified by

\zeta_{i}(v_{j})=\begin{cases}\sigma^{\prime}(v_{j}),&j\leq i;\\
\sigma(v_{j}),&j>i;\end{cases}

such that
\|\zeta_{i}-\zeta_{i-1}\|_{1}
is minimised.

5:

end

for

6:

\xi_{0}=\zeta_{\ell}

/*
Stage 2: Update the rest of variables
*/

7:

Let
\tau^{\prime}={\left.\kern-1.2pt\sigma^{\prime}\right|_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}}}
and suppose that
H_{\Phi^{\tau^{\prime}}}
has connected components
\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{E}_{2},\ldots,\mathcal{E}_{t}
.

8:

for

i\in[t]

do

9:

Let
\xi_{i}\in\Omega
be defined as
\xi_{i}(v)=\begin{cases}\sigma^{\prime}(v),&v\in\left(\mathcal{V}\setminus\bigcup_{j=1}^{t}\mathcal{E}_{j}\right)\cup\left(\bigcup_{j=1}^{i}\mathcal{E}_{j}\right);\\
\zeta_{\ell}(v),&v\in\bigcup_{j=i+1}^{t}\mathcal{E}_{j}.\end{cases}

10:

end

for

11:

return
The path
\sigma=\zeta_{0}\leftrightarrow\cdots\leftrightarrow\zeta_{\ell}=\xi_{0}\leftrightarrow\cdots\leftrightarrow\xi_{r}=\sigma^{\prime}
.

Algorithm 5

Finding a
(\operatorname{poly}(k)\log n)
-path between two satisfying assignments

To prove Theorem
3
, it suffices to show that the output of Algorithm
5
is with high probability a
D
-path in the solution space for
D=2k^{5}\log n
for the inputs
\sigma\sim\mu_{\Omega}
and
\sigma^{\prime}\sim\mu_{\Omega}
. We will not actually require
\sigma\sim\mu_{\Omega}
and
\sigma^{\prime}\sim\mu_{\Omega}
in the proof; instead we will just use the fact that the restrictions of
\sigma
and
\sigma^{\prime}
on
\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}
follow a
(1/k)
-uniform distribution as guaranteed by Lemma
28
, see the proof of Lemma
61
for details.

We need the following two lemmas to establish Theorem
3
.
The first lemma (Lemma
60
) shows that all the truth assignments
\zeta_{i}
,
\xi_{i}
in the algorithm exist and satisfy the formula (i.e. the algorithm is well-defined), implying our constructed path is indeed a valid path comprising only satisfying assignments. The second lemma (Lemma
61
) shows that w.h.p., two adjacent assignments differ by at most
2k^{5}\log n
variables. This result is an application of Corollary
59
.

Lemma 60
.

For any
k
-CNF formula
\Phi
with
n
variables, any
(r,r_{1},0,r_{1})
-marking
(\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}},\emptyset,\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{c}})
of
\Phi
, and any two satisfying assignments
\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}
, Algorithm
5
on these inputs is well-defined in the following sense:

1.

It is always possible to implement Line
4
such that
\zeta_{i}\in\Omega
.

2.

We have
\xi_{i}\in\Omega
for each
i\in[t]
.

Proof.

To prove item
1
, we are going to show that for any partial assignment
X\colon\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}\to\{\mathsf{F},\mathsf{T}\}
, we have
\Pr_{\mu_{\Omega}}(X)>0
and, thus, can extend
X
to some satisfying assignment. If this claim holds, then we can indeed compute the satisfying assignments
\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2},\ldots,\zeta_{\ell}
in Algorithm
5
.
Recall that the distribution
{\left.\kern-1.2pt\mu_{\Omega}\right|_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}}}
is
(1/k)
-uniform, see Lemma
28
. From the definition of
(1/k)
-uniform distribution, we find that an analogous statement to Proposition
10
holds for our
(r,r_{1},0,r_{1})
-marking (here
r=r_{1}-\delta
): for any
v\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{good}}(r)
, any
V\subseteq\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}
with
v\not\in V
, and any
\Lambda\colon V\to\{\mathsf{F},\mathsf{T}\}
, we have

\max\left\{\operatorname{Pr}_{\mu_{\Omega^{\Lambda}}}\left(\left.v\mapsto\mathsf{F}\right|\Lambda\right),\operatorname{Pr}_{\mu_{\Omega}}\left(\left.v\mapsto\mathsf{T}\right|\Lambda\right)\right\}\leq\frac{1}{2}\exp\left(\frac{1}{k}\right).

Thus, by induction on the size of a set
S\subseteq\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}
, we conclude that any assignment
\Lambda\colon S\to\{\mathsf{F},\mathsf{T}\}
has
\Pr_{\mu_{\Omega}}(\Lambda)>0
, finishing the proof of item
1
.

Next consider item
2
. Let
\tau^{\prime}={\left.\kern-1.2pt\sigma^{\prime}\right|_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}}}
as in Algorithm
5
. All clauses that do not appear in
G_{\Phi^{\tau^{\prime}}}
are satisfied by the partial assignment
\tau^{\prime}
. Now consider two satisfying assignments
\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime}
such that
\Lambda(\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}})=\Lambda^{\prime}(\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}})=\tau^{\prime}
. Let
G_{\Phi^{\tau^{\prime}}}
have connected components
\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2},\ldots,\mathcal{C}_{t^{\prime}}
. In particular,
{\left.\kern-1.2pt\Lambda\right|_{{\operatorname{var}(\mathcal{C}_{i})}}}
and
{\left.\kern-1.2pt\Lambda^{\prime}\right|_{\operatorname{var}(\mathcal{C}_{i})}}
each satisfy all clauses in
\mathcal{C}_{i}
. Each clause in
\Phi^{\tau^{\prime}}
is in exactly one connected component
\mathcal{C}_{i}
. Consequently, any assignment
X
such that
{\left.\kern-1.2ptX\right|_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}}}=\tau^{\prime}
and
{\left.\kern-1.2ptX\right|_{\operatorname{var}(\mathcal{C}_{i})}}\in\{{\left.\kern-1.2pt\Lambda\right|_{\operatorname{var}(\mathcal{C}_{i})}},{\left.\kern-1.2pt\Lambda^{\prime}\right|_{\operatorname{var}(\mathcal{C}_{i})}}\}
for all
i\in[t^{\prime}]
is a satisfying assignment (any variables that do not appear in
\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}\cup\left(\bigcup_{i=1}^{t^{\prime}}\operatorname{var}(\mathcal{C}_{i})\right)
can be chosen arbitrarily). We note that there are two types of connected components of
H_{\Phi^{\tau}}
. The first type are those corresponding to
\operatorname{var}(\mathcal{C}_{i})
for some
i\in[t^{\prime}]
. The second type are those connected components with variables in
\mathcal{V}\setminus\left(\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}\cup\left(\bigcup_{i=1}^{t^{\prime}}\operatorname{var}(\mathcal{C}_{i})\right)\right)
. These connected components are singleton and consist of one variable
v
that does not appear in
\Phi^{\tau}
or, equivalently, every clause of
\Phi
containing
v
is satisfied by
\tau
. As a consequence, taking
\Lambda=\zeta_{\ell}
,
\Lambda^{\prime}=\sigma^{\prime}
and
X=\xi_{i}
in the argument above, we conclude that
\xi_{0},\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{t}
are satisfying assignments by construction in Algorithm
5
and item
2
holds.
∎

Lemma 61
.

There is an integer
k_{0}\geq 3
such that, for any integer
k\geq k_{0}
, any density
\alpha\leq 2^{(r_{1}-3\delta)k}
, the following holds w.h.p. over the choice of
\Phi=\Phi(k,n,\lfloor\alpha n\rfloor)
. In Algorithm
5
with inputs the formula
\Phi
, an
(r,r_{1},0,r_{1})
-marking of
\Phi
and the two satisfying assignments
\sigma
and
\sigma^{\prime}
, with probability at least
1-1/n
over the choices
\sigma\sim\mu_{\Omega},\sigma^{\prime}\sim\mu_{\Omega}
, we have

1.

\|\zeta_{i}-\zeta_{i-1}\|_{1}\leq 2k^{5}\log n
for all
i\in[\ell]
;

2.

\|\xi_{i}-\xi_{i-1}\|_{1}\leq 2k^{5}\log n
for all
i\in[t]
.

Proof.

Let
\Phi
and
(\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}},\emptyset,\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{a}})
be the first two inputs of Algorithm
5
, and let
v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{\ell}
be the variables in
\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}
in the order considered in Algorithm
5
. Let
\sigma\sim\mu_{\Omega}
and
\sigma^{\prime}\sim\mu_{\Omega}
. Let
\sigma=\zeta_{0}\leftrightarrow\cdots\leftrightarrow\zeta_{\ell}=\xi_{0}\leftrightarrow\cdots\leftrightarrow\xi_{r}=\sigma^{\prime}
be the path between
\sigma
and
\sigma^{\prime}
output by Algorithm
5
. In light of Lemma
60
, the assignments
\zeta_{0},\zeta_{1},\ldots,\zeta_{\ell},\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{r}
are satisfying assignments of
\Phi
. We also note that the set of marked variables
\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}
is
r_{1}
-distributed and does not contain bad variables by Definition
8
. We are going to apply Corollary
59
with
V=\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}
several times in this proof. In view of Lemma
28
, the distribution
{\left.\kern-1.2pt\mu_{\Omega}\right|_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}}}
is
(1/k)
-uniform, and this will be relevant when applying Corollary
59
. We prove that Item
1
holds with probability at least
1-1/(2n)
and that Item
2
holds with probability
1-1/(2n)
, so the result follows from a union bound.

Item
1
. Let
i\in[\ell]
and let
\tau_{i}
be the restriction of
\zeta_{i}
to
\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}
. By construction,
\tau_{i}
agrees with
\sigma^{\prime}
on
v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{i}
and it agrees with
\sigma
on
v_{i+1},v_{i+2},\ldots,v_{\ell}
. Let
\Lambda_{i}
denote the restriction of
\tau_{i}
on
\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}\setminus\{v_{i}\}
, which agrees with
\zeta_{i}
and
\zeta_{i-1}
on
\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}\setminus\{v_{i}\}
. Recall that, by definition,
\zeta_{i}
is the satisfying assignment that extends
\tau_{i}
that minimises
\|\zeta_{i}-\zeta_{i-1}\|_{1}
, see Algorithm
5
. We consider the connected components of
G_{\Phi^{\Lambda_{i}}}
, which can be seen as CNF formulae with variables in
\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{c}}\cup\{v_{i}\}
due to the fact that all marked variables other than
v_{i}
are set by
\Lambda_{i}
. Each one of these connected components are satisfied as CNF formulae by the assignments
\zeta_{i}
and
\zeta_{i-1}
. We conclude that
\zeta_{i}
and
\zeta_{i-1}
agree on the variables of all these connected components except for those variables in the connected component of the clauses containing
v_{i}
, where
\zeta_{i}
and
\zeta_{i-1}
may disagree. Let us denote this connected component by
C_{v_{i}}
, which is empty when all the clauses containing
v_{i}
are satisfied by
\Lambda_{i}
. We have
\|\zeta_{i}-\zeta_{i-1}\|_{1}\leq k\lvert C_{v_{i}}\rvert
, where the factor
k
comes from the fact that each clause has at most
k
variables. We now bound the size of
C_{v_{i}}
. Since the restrictions of
\sigma
and
\sigma^{\prime}
to
\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}
follow
{\left.\kern-1.2pt\mu_{\Omega}\right|_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}}}
, which is
(1/k)
-uniform, we find, by Definition
12
, that
\tau_{i}
also follows an
(1/k)
-uniform distribution over the assignments
\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}\to\{\mathsf{F},\mathsf{T}\}
. Let us denote this distribution by
\mu_{i}
. Then
\Lambda_{i}\sim{\left.\kern-1.2pt\mu_{i}\right|_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}\setminus\{v_{i}\}}}
and, by Corollary
59
with
V=\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}
,
\Lambda=\Lambda_{i}
and
\mu=\mu_{i}
, with probability at least
1-n^{-k}
over the choice
\Lambda_{i}\sim{\left.\kern-1.2pt\mu_{i}\right|_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}\setminus\{v_{i}\}}}
, the connected component
C_{v_{i}}\subset G_{\Phi^{\Lambda_{i}}}
containing
v_{i}
has at most
2k^{4}\log n
clauses. Thus, with probability at least
1-n^{-k}
, we have
\|\zeta_{i}-\zeta_{i-1}\|_{1}\leq k\lvert C_{v_{i}}\rvert\leq 2k^{5}\log n
. By a union bound over
i\in[\ell]
and the fact that
k\geq 3
and
\ell\leq n
, we conclude that, with probability at least
1-1/n^{2}
, we have
\|\zeta_{i}-\zeta_{i-1}\|_{1}\leq 2k^{5}\log n
for all
i\in[\ell]
.

Item
2
. Let
\tau^{\prime}=\sigma^{\prime}|_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}}
as in Algorithm
5
. By construction,
\xi_{0}=\zeta_{\ell}
and
\xi_{t}=\sigma^{\prime}
agree with
\tau^{\prime}
on
\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}
. Since
\sigma^{\prime}\sim\mu_{\Omega}
, we have
\tau^{\prime}\sim{\left.\kern-1.2pt\mu_{\Omega}\right|_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}}}
, which is
(1/k)
-uniform by Lemma
28
. In view of Corollary
59
for
V=\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}
,
\Lambda=\tau^{\prime}
and
\mu={\left.\kern-1.2pt\mu_{\Omega}\right|_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}}}
, with probability at least
1-n^{-k}
, all of the connected components of
G_{\Phi^{\tau^{\prime}}}
, have size at most
2k^{4}\log n
. Thus, all the connected components of
H_{\Phi^{\tau^{\prime}}}
have size at most
2k^{5}\log n
. By construction, see Line
9
in Algorithm
5
, the assignments
\xi_{i-1}
and
\xi_{i}
agree on the variables in all the connected components of
H_{\Phi^{\tau^{\prime}}}
except for the variables in the
i
-th connected component, where they may disagree. Thus, they disagree on at most
2k^{5}\log n
variables. This gives the desired result.
∎

We can now complete the proof of Theorem
3
.

Theorem
3
.

There is
k_{0}\geq 3
and a polynomial
p(k)
with non-negative integer coefficients such that, for any integer
k\geq k_{0}
, and for any positive real
\alpha\leq 2^{0.227k}
, the following claim holds with high probability over the choice of a random
k
-CNF formula
\Phi=\Phi(k,n,\lfloor\alpha n\rfloor)
. Two satisfying assignments chosen uniformly at random are
p(k)\log(n)
-connected with probability at least
1-1/n
.

Proof.

Since
\alpha\leq 2^{0.227k}\leq 2^{(r_{1}-3\delta)k}/k^{3}\leq 2^{(r_{1}-\delta)k}/k^{3}
for large enough
k
, w.h.p. over the choice of
\Phi
, there is an
(r,r_{1},0,r_{1})
-marking
(\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}},\emptyset,\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{c}})
of
\Phi
, see Lemma
27
. We run Algorithm
5
with inputs
\Phi
, and the associated marking
(\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}},\emptyset,\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{c}})
. W.h.p. over the choice of
\Phi
, Lemma
61
holds. Therefore, with probability at least
1-1/n
over the choice of two random satisfying assignments
\sigma\sim\mu_{\Omega}
and
\sigma^{\prime}\sim\mu_{\Omega}
, the output path of Algorithm
5
is well-defined by Lemma
60
and satisfies that
\|\zeta_{i}-\zeta_{i-1}\|_{1}\leq 2k^{5}\log n
for all
i\in[\ell]
and
\|\xi_{i}-\xi_{i-1}\|_{1}\leq 2k^{5}\log n
for all
i\in[t]
by Lemma
61
.
Hence, it is a
D
-path in the solution space
\Omega
for
D=2k^{5}\log n
as we wanted.
∎

10.2
Proof of Theorem
5

We next show
O(\log n)
-looseness for all variables with high probability over
(\Phi,\sigma)
for random
k
-CNF instances
\Phi
and uniformly random satisfying assignment
\sigma\in\Omega
. Consequently, in an
algorithmic
regime where
\alpha\ll 2^{ck}
for some
c<1
, we resolve a conjecture of
[
1
]
.
Our proof exploits Corollary
59
on the size of the connected components of
\Phi^{\Lambda}
. It is important in our arguments that every variable in the formula is
flippable
.

Definition 62
.

Let
\Phi=\Phi(k,n,m)
be a random
k
-CNF. A variable
v\in\mathcal{V}
is flippable if there exists a pair of satisfying assignments
(X,Y)
to
\Phi
, in one of which
X(v)=\mathsf{F}
and in the other
Y(v)=\mathsf{T}
.

Lemma 63
.

For
\alpha<2^{k-2}
, with high probability over the choice of
\Phi=\Phi(k,m,n)
, all variables in
\Phi
are flippable.

Proof.

Observe that we can define an NAE-SAT problem based on
\Phi
. By definition, any NAE-satisfying assignment ensures that every clause contains at least one satisfied literal and at least one unsatisfied literal. By Theorem 2 in
[
4
]
, with high probability
\Phi
is NAE-satisfiable.
Consequently, we can find some assignment
\sigma
that NAE-satisfies
\Phi
with high probability, and then the opposite assignment
\overline{\sigma}
also NAE-satisfies
\Phi
by the symmetry of NAE-SAT solutions. In particular, both
\sigma
and
\overline{\sigma}
are solutions to the original SAT formula
\Phi
. Observe that for every variable
v\in V
we have
X(v)=\mathsf{T}
and
X(v)=\mathsf{F}
in exactly one of
\sigma,\overline{\sigma}
and thus, with high probability, every variable in
\Phi
is flippable.
∎

Lemma 64
.

For any variable
v\in\mathcal{V}
and any partial assignment
X\colon\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}\setminus\{v\}\to\{\mathsf{F},\mathsf{T}\}
, we have

\operatorname{Pr}_{\mu_{\Omega}}(v\mapsto\mathsf{F}|X)>0\quad\text{and}\quad\operatorname{Pr}_{\mu_{\Omega}}(v\mapsto\mathsf{T}|X)>0.

Proof.

We prove
\operatorname{Pr}_{\mu_{\Omega}}(v\to\mathsf{F}|X)>0
; the proof of
\operatorname{Pr}_{\mu_{\Omega}}(v\to\mathsf{T}|X)>0
is analogous. We distinguish two cases.

The first case is when
v
is a good variable. Lemma
28
gives
\operatorname{Pr}_{\mu_{\Omega}}\left(v\mapsto\mathsf{F}|X,\Lambda_{\bad}\right)\geq 1-\exp(1/k)/2>0
for any satisfying assignment of the bad clauses
\Lambda_{\bad}
. Thus, we have
\operatorname{Pr}_{\mu_{\Omega}}\left(v\mapsto\mathsf{F}|X\right)>0
.

The second case is when
v
is a bad variable. By Lemma
63
there exists a satisfying assignment
\sigma
with
\sigma(v)=\mathsf{F}
.
Let
\Lambda_{\bad}={\left.\kern-1.2pt\sigma\right|_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{bad}}}}
be the assignment on bad variables and so in particular
\operatorname{Pr}_{\mu_{\Omega}}(\Lambda_{\bad})>0
.
Then by Lemma
28
we have
\operatorname{Pr}_{\mu_{\Omega}}\left(X|\Lambda_{\bad}\right)\geq(1-\exp(1/k)/2)^{\lvert\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}\rvert}>0
. This implies that
\operatorname{Pr}_{\mu_{\Omega}}(X,\Lambda_{\bad})>0

and in particular
\operatorname{Pr}_{\mu_{\Omega}}(v\mapsto\mathsf{F},X)>0
, so
\operatorname{Pr}_{\mu_{\Omega}}(v\mapsto\mathsf{F}|X)>0
.
∎

We can now prove Theorem
5
with the help of Corollary
59
.

Theorem
5
.

There is
k_{0}\geq 3
such that, for any integer
k\geq k_{0}
, and for any positive real
\alpha\leq 2^{0.227k}
, the random
k
-CNF formula
\Phi(k,n,\lfloor\alpha n\rfloor)
is
\operatorname{poly}(k)\log(n)
-loose.

Proof.

Note that
2^{0.227k}\leq 2^{(r_{1}-3\delta)k}\leq 2^{(r_{1}-\delta)k}/k^{3}
for large enough
k
. Thus, w.h.p. over the choice of
\Phi
, there is an
(r,r_{1},\emptyset,r_{1})
-marking
(\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}},\emptyset,\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{c}})
of
\Phi
, see Lemma
26
. The distribution
{\left.\kern-1.2pt\mu_{\Omega}\right|_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}}}
is
(1/k)
-uniform by Lemma
28
. Hence, Corollary
59
holds for
V=\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}
and
\mu={\left.\kern-1.2pt\mu_{\Omega}\right|_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}}}
. Let
v
be a variable of
\Phi
. Let
\sigma\sim\mu_{\Omega}
and let
\Lambda
be the restriction of
\sigma
to
\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{m}}\setminus\{v\}
. Then, with probability at least
1-n^{-k}
, the connected components of
G_{\Phi^{\Lambda}}
have size at most
2k^{4}\log n
. Let
\mathcal{C}^{\Lambda}_{j}
be the connected component containing the variable
v
, which is empty if all clauses containing
v
are satisfied. Let
\omega
be the negation of
\sigma(v)
. By Lemma
64
, we have
\operatorname{Pr}_{\mu_{\Omega}}(v\mapsto\omega|\Lambda)>0
. Therefore, there is an assignment
Y
of the variables in
\operatorname{var}(\mathcal{C}^{\Lambda}_{j})
that satisfies the clauses in
\mathcal{C}^{\Lambda}_{j}
and has
Y(v)=\omega
. We construct the assignment
\sigma^{\prime}
that has
\sigma^{\prime}(v)=\omega
, agrees with
Y
in
\operatorname{var}(\mathcal{C}^{\Lambda}_{j})
and agrees with
\sigma
in the rest of the variables of
\Phi
. In particular, this assignment agrees with
\Lambda
and satisfies each one of the connected components of
\Phi^{\Lambda}
. Thus,
\sigma^{\prime}
is a satisfying assignment of
\Phi
. Moreover, w.h.p.
\sigma^{\prime}
differs with
\sigma
in at most
2k^{5}\log n
variables (the variables in
\operatorname{var}(\mathcal{C}^{\Lambda}_{j})
). We have shown that, w.h.p. over the choice of
\Phi
, with probability at least
1-n^{-k}
a random assignment
\sigma\sim\mu_{\Omega}
is
(2k^{5}\log n)
-loose, so the statement follows.
∎

Appendix A
Proof of Lemma
20

In this section we prove Lemma
20
. Recall that this result is
[
24
, Lemma 8.16]
with a less restrictive bound on the density of the formula and a more restrictive definition of good variables/clauses, see Section
4
for details. Moreover, the obtained upper bound on the number of bad clauses in our version of
[
24
, Lemma 8.16]
is tighter. The original proof of Lemma
20
given in
[
24
, Section 8]
is split into a sequence of results on random formulae. Here we restate
some of these results —
only those
whose statement needs to change as a consequence of our definition of good variables/clauses and the tighter upper bound. We also explain how these changes affect the proofs
